## Page 1


REPRESENTATION THEORY OF THE GELFAND QUIVER
AND HARISH-CHANDRA MODULES FOR SL2(R)
IGOR BURBAN AND WASSILIJ GNEDIN
Abstract. In 1970, Gelfand posed the problem of classifying the indecomposable ob-
jects in a representation category equivalent to the principal block of Harish-Chandra
modules for SL2(R); explicit solutions were obtained by Bondarenko, and, independently,
Crawley-Boevey. In this article, we give a complete answer to Gelfand’s problem from a
derived category perspective. We classify indecomposable objects in the bounded derived
category of nilpotent representations of the Gelfand quiver in terms of band and string
complexes, and determine their images under the derived Auslander-Reiten translation,
the sign involution, and the contragredient duality. The four main combinatorial classes
are characterized in Lie-theoretic as well as homological terms. For the abelian category
of nilpotent representations, we provide projective resolutions, standard homological in-
variants and explicit representation matrices of all indecomposables. Our approach can
be extended to arrow ideal completions of path algebras of skew-gentle quivers.
1. Introduction
In his ICM talk in 1970, I. Gelfand [16] raised the question of finding an explicit de-
scription of the indecomposable complex finite-dimensional nilpotent representations of
the quiver
−

b+
b−a−= b+a+.
(1.1)
The motivation to study this problem comes from the fact that the category of such
representations of (1.1) is equivalent to the principal block of the category of Harish–
Chandra modules for the Lie group SL2(R).
Already in 1959 Zhelobenko [38] showed that the principal block of the category of
Harish-Chandra modules for SL2(C) is equivalent to the category of complex finite-dimen-
sional nilpotent representations of the quiver
◦
◦
x
b
a
bx = xa = 0.
(1.2)
A description of the corresponding indecomposable representations was obtained by Gel-
fand and Ponomarev in 1968; see [17]. Their work was very influential for the further
development of the representation theory of associative algebras.
However, the case of the quiver (1.1) turned out to be more complicated. Quoting [16]:
“The question of the classification of the objects of this category is apparently solvable
but leads to considerable difficulties”.
In 1973 Nazarova and Roiter proved that the category of finite-dimensional nilpotent
representations of (1.1) is representation-tame, reducing the problem of description of
its indecomposable objects to a certain problem of linear algebra (matrix problem) [29].

However, the correct combinatorics of indecomposables were obtained only in the end
of the 1980s by Bondarenko [4, 5] and Crawley-Boevey [12] by substantially different
methods. Further solutions to Gelfand’s problem were given by Deng [14] and Iyama [20].
In 1981 Khoroshkin obtained a quiver description of the principal blocks of Harish–
Chandra modules for the Lorentz groups SO+(n, 1) proving they are representation-tame
for all n [22]. In case n = 2m, this quiver is given as follows.

In [9] the first-named author together with Drozd showed that the Khoroshkin quivers are
even derived-tame.
Although the indecomposable representations of the Gelfand quiver have been classified
via prior approaches, a systematic treatment via the derived category has not been carried
out.
In this article, we describe the indecomposable objects in the derived category of nilpo-
tent representations of the Gelfand quiver, and determine their images under the derived
Auslander–Reiten translation and contragredient duality. This approach has two advan-
tages over prior methods: first, the techniques are amenable to generalizations to a larger
class of derived-tame algebras; second, the results on the derived category yield a con-
ceptual framework to study the original, abelian category. Following this approach, we
provide a detailed solution to Gelfand’s problem, including projective resolutions, repre-
sentation matrices, homological invariants and functorial properties of all indecomposable
nilpotent representations.
Main results. For the sake of simplicity of the presentation, we fix an algebraically closed
field k as base field. The category of finite-dimensional nilpotent k-linear representations
of the Gelfand quiver will be denoted by rep Λ, its bounded derived category by Db(rep Λ).
We give approximate descriptions of certain combinatorial notions, called bands and strings
of Db(rep Λ).
• A periodic word w is given by a periodic sequence (xi)i∈Z, where xi is a decorated
number
→ni or
←ni with a positive integer ni ∈N for each index i, and a minimal
periodic part of w has as many left-oriented as right-oriented numbers. Any band
ω of Db(rep Λ) has the form (d, w, m, λ) consisting of an integer d ∈Z, a periodic
word w, a number m ∈N and a scalar λ ∈k∗.
• Any finite word w is a sequence (α, x1, x2, . . . xℓ, β) with ends α, β ∈{⋆, ⋄}, and
certain decorated numbers in between. A finite word is usual, special or bispecial
if it has 0, 1 or 2 ends of type ⋄, respectively. A string of Db(rep Λ) has one of the
following forms, where d ∈Z is arbitrary.
– A usual string (d, w) with a usual, asymmetric word w.
– A special string (d, w, ε) with a special word w and a sign ε ∈{+, −}.
– A bispecial string (d, ε1, w, ε2) with a bispecial word w and two signs ε1, ε2.
In particular, strings are defined using only discrete parameters, while bands depend on a
continuous parameter from the base field. To any band or string ω, we associate a ‘gluing
diagram’. Applying certain combinatorial ‘gluing rules’ we process such a diagram into an

The second main result involves the following three notions.
• For a complex X• from Db(rep Λ) we define its defect as the number
δ(X•) =

• The Gelfand quiver admits a vertical symmetry interchanging the signs + and −
in vertices and arrows. This symmetry induces a k-algebra automorphism of Λ,
and thus an auto-equivalence σ of Db(rep Λ) such that σ2 ∼= 1.
• By known results [21, 36], the category Db(rep Λ) admits an auto-equivalence called
the Auslander-Reiten translation τ, which gives rise to a Serre functor S = τ ◦[1].
It turns out that these homological notions align well with the four combinatorial classes
of indecomposable objects defined above.

The first statement motivates the terminology ‘defect’ and indicates that the functor
τ is essentially determined by the Betti invariants of the complex X•. The intuition for
the first set of equivalences in (2) comes from the fact that the defect corresponds to the
number of ends of type ⋆of the underlying word w, while σ simply flips the signs in ω
if it has any sign data. The first set of equivalences yields a homological characterization
of the four combinatorial classes. If k = C, this characterization can be interpreted in
terms of the derived category of the principal blocks of Harish-Chandra modules over
SL(2, R) as the defect as well as the involution admit a Lie-theoretic origin. The second
set of equivalences in (2) follows from the first statement and describes the four classes
in terms of natural functors on Db(rep Λ). This result implies that bispecial strings form

In addition to these functors, the category Db(rep Λ) admits a contravariant involutive
auto-equivalence (−)‡, which originates from contragredient duality on blocks of Harish-
Chandra modules equivalent to the category rep Λ. The next result describes the action
of the aforementioned functors on indecomposable objects in Db(rep Λ).
Result C (Proposition 10.1, Theorems 10.8 and 10.19). Let F be an auto-equivalence
of Db(rep Λ) given by the involution σ, Auslander-Reiten translation τ, or contragredient
duality (−)‡. For any band or string ω we determine the band respectively string F(ω)
such that there is an isomorphism F(X•(ω)) ∼= X•(F(ω)) in Db(rep Λ).

The involution σ and the duality endofunctor (−)‡ preserve the abelian category rep Λ,
in contrast to the derived Auslander-Reiten translation. Therefore, one may specialize
part of Result C to describe these functors in terms of band and string representations
(Theorem 13.2).
Next, we describe certain homological invariants of indecomposable objects in rep Λ.
Result E (Theorem 13.5 and Corollary 13.13). For any band or string ω of rep Λ we
determine the projective and the injective dimension, the top-, the socle- and the Jordan–
H¨older-multiplicities, and the Euler characteristic of the indecomposable representation
M(ω).
These invariants can be computed using only the numerical data underlying ω. For
instance, for a band ω = (w, m, λ), the indecomposable representation M(ω) has projective
and injective dimension one, top M ∼= soc M ∼= (S+ ⊕S−)mk where 2k denotes the period
of the word w, dim M = (mn, mn, mn) where n is the sum of all numbers in a minimal
periodic part of w, and vanishing Euler characteristic.
Finally, we give an explicit description of the indecomposable representations in the
category rep Λ, addressing Gelfand’s original question.
In this course, the description
of the quiver representation of M(ω) can be built from certain cyclic representations
determined by the combinatorial data of its band or string ω.

Result F (Propositions 14.8, 14.14, 14.21). For any band or string ω of rep Λ we provide
an explicit description of vector spaces and matrices of the quiver representation of M(ω).
There are analogues of the results above for an arbitrary base field k. We note that the
main results of this article extend to any Khoroshkin quiver, and to principal blocks of
the Lorentz groups SO+(n, 1) for any n. The underlying approach applies more generally
to the arrow ideal completion of the path algebra of any skew-gentle quiver.
Outline and approach. In the following, we briefly describe the structure and the main
methods of this article.
The proof-dependencies between the sections are depicted in
Figure 1.6.
Sections 2 to 4 form the first part of this work, which provides an overview of the repre-
sentation theory of the Gelfand quiver without using heavy combinatorial machinery. In
Section 2, we give an account of the Lie-theoretic origins of the category rep Λ of nilpo-
tent representations of the Gelfand quiver, as well as the involution σ and contragredient
duality (−)‡. Section 3 yields natural examples of indecomposable representations of the
Gelfand quiver, in particular, a classification of its cyclic representations. In Section 4, we
consider homological properties of the ring Λ, introduce the main functors on the derived
category Db(rep Λ) and relate the derived Auslander-Reiten translation τ to the defect,
proving Result B (1).
The primary goal of the second part of the paper, Sections 5 to 10, is to provide
a classification of indecomposable objects in the category Db(rep Λ). Following [9], our
method is based on the use of the category of gluing triples gtfd(Λ) and the category of
regular representations rep∼
=(XΛ) of a certain combinatorial datum XΛ. These categories
arise together with certain natural functors

## Page 6

IGOR BURBAN AND WASSILIJ GNEDIN
In the third and last part of this article, we return our focus on the abelian category
rep Λ. Section 11 addresses the question how to identify the band or string complexes which
are projective resolutions of objects in rep Λ. Some necessary conditions are provided using
the language of gluing triples. They are verified to be sufficient by going through the list
of candidate band and string complexes.
In Section 12, we introduce the bands and
strings of rep Λ in a compact notation, and describe their associated projective resolutions
from scratch. An intermediate step in the gluing process of a band or string resolution
is its ‘cyclification’, a simpler projective resolution with the same Betti numbers. The
cyclification already determines the main homological invariants computed in Section 13.
Finally, in Section 14 we compute a quiver representation of the homology of each band
and string resolution. A basis of the latter can be identified with a natural basis of the
homology of the associated cyclification.
The main results of this article are stated in a self-contained manner. In particular,
readers interested only in results on the derived category Db(rep Λ) may start with Sec-
tion 8, followed by Section 10 skipping the proofs. In the same spirit, readers interested
only in results on the abelian category rep Λ may begin with Section 12, and then consult
Section 13 on invariants and functors, or Section 14 on the final description of indecom-
posable representations. Both reading paths presume familiarity with the notions below.

2. Harish-Chandra modules for SL2(R) and the Gelfand quiver
This section provides some Lie-theoretic background motivating the study of the rep-
resentation theory of the Gelfand quiver. First, we recall that the category of Harish-
Chandra modules for SL2(R) admits a block decomposition such that each block is equiv-
alent to the category of C-linear nilpotent representations of the one-loop quiver, the cyclic
quiver with two vertices or the Gelfand quiver. Then we consider a natural involution and
the contragredient duality on Harish-Chandra modules, as well as their incarnations σ and
(−)‡ on the category rep Λ of nilpotent representations of the Gelfand quiver.

For further background on Harish-Chandra modules, we refer to Schmid [33] and Wal-
lach [37].
3. Elementary representation theory of the Gelfand quiver
In this section, we gather a few basic observations on finitely generated modules over
the Gelfand order Λ. In particular, we recall the description of lattices over the Gelfand
order, introduce natural bases of its indecomposable projective modules, determine all
cyclic representations as well as all bricks in rep Λ up to isomorphism, and consider the
smallest example of a one-parameter family (Mλ)λ∈k of indecomposable representations.
Finally, we sketch the relationship of the Gelfand order to an associated gentle order first
considered by Khoroshkin [22].
Cyclic representations will reappear as elementary ingredients of a gluing procedure in
Section 14, where their classification will be recovered in terms of projective resolutions.
3.1. Lattices over the Gelfand order. As before, let k be an algebraically closed field,
R = kJtK, m = Rt and Λ be the Gelfand order (2.16). Note that the ring R is isomorphic
to the center of the matrix algebra Λ via the assignment r
diag(r, r, r) for any element
r ∈R.
Definition 3.1. A finitely generated Λ-module L is called a lattice if it is free as R-module.
We will frequently use the indecomposable Λ-lattices

The natural inclusion of I⋆into P⋆will be denoted by ι. The modules P⋆, P+ and P−
are precisely the indecomposable projective Λ-modules up to isomorphism.
Remark 3.3. Any indecomposable Λ-lattice is isomorphic to one of the four lattices above.
More precisely, the Gelfand order Λ admits a hereditary envelope Γ, see (5.1), such that
any indecomposable Λ-lattice is a direct summand of Λ ⊕Γ; see [32, Theorem B and
Example 4]. More generally, any nodal order admits a hereditary envelope (see [9, 11] for
the definition), and a similar classification of indecomposable lattices holds true by [32, 10].

3.3. Cyclic modules and their resolutions. For a finitely generated module M the
intersection rad M of its maximal left submodules can be identified with (rad Λ)M, where
rad Λ denotes the Jacobson radical of the ring Λ.
Let us recall that a non-zero Λ-module M is called cyclic if any of the following equiv-
alent conditions holds.
(1) There is an element v ∈M such that M = Λv.
(2) The k-linear space top M = M/ rad M is one-dimensional.
(3) M is isomorphic to a quotient of an indecomposable projective Λ-module.

For a finite-dimensional M we denote by ℓℓ(M) its Loewy length, that is, the smallest
number p ∈N such that radp M = 0. The Loewy length can also be expressed as the
nilpotency degree of the operator t on M. The socle of M will be denoted by soc M and
is the submodule of elements of M annihilated by rad Λ.
Corollary 3.7. Any cyclic finite-dimensional Λ-modules M and M′ are isomorphic if
and only if top M ∼= top M′ and dim M = dim M′ if and only if top M ∼= top M′, soc M ∼=
soc M′ and ℓℓ(M) = ℓℓ(M′).

Definition 3.10. An object X of an additive category C is a brick if EndC(X) is a division

Proposition 3.11. Up to isomorphism, the bricks of the category rep Λ are given by the
representations of Lemma 3.9 without the representation B⋆.
Proof. It is straightforward to check that each of the eleven representations in question is a
brick. Vice versa, let M ∈rep Λ be a brick. Then the left multiplication t·: M →M defines
a non-invertible endomorphism, which implies that tM = 0. Hence, M is a module over
the finite-dimensional k-algebra Λ/tΛ. Since Λ/tΛ ∼= ¯Λ/¯Λ(b−a−)¯Λ, Lemma 3.9 implies
the claim.
□
Theorem 2.7 and Proposition 3.11 recover the following statement.
Corollary 3.12. An object M of the C-linear category HC(g, K) has one-dimensional
endomorphism ring if and only if the following hold: M belongs to one of the blocks from
Theorem 2.7, dim CMn ≤1 for any n ∈Z, and for any l ∈Z with xl = yl = 0 it follows
that Ml−1 = 0 or Ml+1 = 0.
□

For the remainder of this section, we assume that char (k) ̸= 2.
In this case, the
representation theory of the algebra B is closely related to that of the Gelfand order via
a skew group algebra as intermediate link.

Moreover, there is an involution on ΛC2 interchanging signs in its paths, which induces an

Theorem 3.15. In the setup above, the following statements hold.
(1) Any indecomposable object M in rep Λ is isomorphic to a direct summand of the
restriction ΛN of an indecomposable object N from rep ΛC2.
(2) For any indecomposable object N from rep ΛC2 the following holds.
• If N is not eσ-invariant, then ΛN is indecomposable, σ-invariant and ΛN ∼=
Λ(eσ(N)).
• If N is eσ-invariant, then ΛN ∼= M ⊕σ(M) for an indecomposable non-σ-
invariant object M from rep Λ .
Together with the previously stated classification results on rep B, this theorem can be
used to conclude that the category rep Λ is representation-tame if char (k) ̸= 2. While the
decomposition of ΛN can be determined for any individual eσ-invariant indecomposable
object N in rep ΛC2 using the methods in [22], describing the decompositions uniformly
for all such N is a non-trivial task.

Remark 3.16. If k = C, the category rep B, and thus the category rep ΛC2, is equivalent

4. Basic homological algebra of the Gelfand quiver
This section collects some homological properties of the derived category Db(rep Λ) of
nilpotent representations of the Gelfand order. We observe that Λ is a self-opposite ring
of global dimension two, introduce an elementary duality endofunctor (−)† on Db(rep Λ),
and relate it to the derived versions of the involution σ and the contragredient duality
(−)‡ introduced before. Then we provide a factorization of the derived Auslander-Reiten
translation on Db(rep Λ) in terms of a spherical auto-equivalence TS⋆associated to the
simple module S⋆(Proposition 4.14). This result is crucial to prove that τ-periodicity,
τ 2-invariance and vanishing defect are all equivalent conditions (Proposition 4.21, Re-
sult B (1)). This section prepares the ground for the explicit description of the functors
on the derived category in Section 10.

5. The gluing method
Following [9], we recall a categorical framework in this section, the category of gluing
triples gt(Λ). Its construction is based on a realization of the Gelfand order as pullback
ring in a diagram of certain structurally simpler rings A, B and Γ, as shown on the left.

The pullback diagram gives rise to a diagram of homotopy categories and functors. In
particular, to any complex X• of projective Λ-modules we may associate a complex Y•
of projective Γ-modules, a complex S• of projective A-modules and an isomorphism ϑ of
complexes of projective B-modules. The triple F(X•) = (S•, Y•, ϑ) yields an example of
an object in the category gt(Λ). In fact, the complex X• can be reconstructed from this
triple. The general notion of a triple is obtained relaxing the condition that Y•, S• and ϑ
originate from a complex of projective Λ-modules. A slight variation of this construction
allows to relate Db(rep Λ) to gtfd(Λ) in Corollary 5.20.
Given an arbitrary gluing triple γ, the main novelty of this section is an explicit con-
struction of a complex X′

in the later combinatorics of band and string complexes.

It is straightforward to define the direct sum of gluing triples. In particular, the category
gt(Λ) is k-linear and additive.
Remark 5.7.
(1) There is a notion of pullback category or comma category asso-
ciated to the pair of functors in (5.6), which is defined in the same way as the
category of gluing triples except that the morphism ϑ of an object (S•, Y•, ϑ) does
not have to be an isomorphism.
(2) In fact, the category gt(Λ) depends on the choice of the overring Γ and the choice
of a common two-sided ideal I of Λ and Γ, but we suppress these dependencies in
the notation.
In the next remark, we recall certain useful features of semiperfect rings.
Remark 5.8. For any semiperfect ring S, the following statements are true.
(1) Any complex in Hotb(proj S) is isomorphic to a minimal complex, that is, a complex
(P•, ∂) such that im ∂i ⊆rad Pi−1 for each degree i ∈Z.
(2) A morphism between minimal complexes in Hotb(proj S) is a homotopy equivalence
if and only if it is an isomorphism of complexes.
The notion of a minimal complex has a counterpart in the category of gluing triples
introduced in the next statement.
Lemma 5.9. Any object in gt(Λ) is isomorphic to a minimal triple, that is, a gluing triple
(S•, Y•, ϑ) satisfying any of the following, equivalent conditions.
(1) The complex S• has zero differentials, the complex Y• is minimal and the morphism
ϑ is an isomorphism of complexes.
(2) The complexes S• and Y• are minimal.
(3) The complex Y• is minimal and ϑ is an isomorphism of complexes.

Remark 5.13. Any morphism (ϕ, ψ) in the category gt(Λ) is given by a pair of homotopy
classes (g, h) of morphisms of complexes. There is a natural definition of a morphism
G(g, h) of complexes of Λ-modules using the universal property of the pullback. However,
if g and h are homotopic to zero, G(g, h) may not be homotopic to zero, in general.
Therefore, G does not extend to a functor in a natural way.
We recall a weakened version of the notion of an equivalence functor [2, Definition 1.1.4].

Corollary 5.18. The category gt(Λ) has the Krull-Remak-Schmidt property.
Proof. We recall that the category Db(rep Λ) has the Krull-Remak-Schmidt property,
see [9, Corollary A.6]. Since the functor F is full and reflects the zero object, the en-
domorphism ring of any indecomposable object in gt(Λ) is local.
□

6. Reduction to matrix problem
In this section, we first recall the combinatorics of the category Db(rep Γ) of the hered-
itary envelope Γ of the Gelfand order Λ. These combinatorics motivate the definition
of the category of regular representations rep∼
=(XΛ). We describe the relationship of the
former to the category gtfd(Λ), and conclude the categorical reductions for the category
Db(rep Λ). The overall picture is summarized in Theorem 6.18, its essential consequences

ments hold.
(1) A Γ-module N is finite-dimensional and indecomposable if and only if N is iso-

To derive some consequences of the last theorem, we observe a simple categorical fact.
Lemma 6.19. Let C be a Krull-Remak-Schmidt category, D an additive category and
F : C

given by a full subcategory K of C. Then F restricts to a detecting functor

7. Bondarenko’s canonical forms for bunches of skew-gentle type
In [4, 5], Bondarenko described the canonical normal forms for certain tame matrix
problems. Here, we specialize Bondarenko’s classification results to a more narrow class
of matrix problems over combinatorial structures called bunches of skew-gentle type. A
prototypical example of such a matrix problem is the four subspace problem.
In this
section, we restrict our focus to matrix representations with invertible matrices, introduce
bands and strings ω of X and recall Bondarenko’s canonical forms M(ω), which provide a
complete classification of matrix representations of X (Theorem 7.25). In the next sections,


## Page 43


REPRESENTATION THEORY OF THE GELFAND QUIVER
43
this result is applied to the bunch of skew-gentle type XΛ and thus forms the foundation
for the classification of indecomposable objects in Db(rep Λ).
7.1. Bunches of skew-gentle type and matrix problems. A chain is a partially
ordered set such that any two elements are comparable. By a two-point link we mean a
partially ordered set given by two incomparable elements.
Definition 7.1. A bunch of skew-gentle type (X, ≥, ≈) is given by the following data.
(1) A disjoint union X = E ∪F called the set of labels such that the set of horizontal
labels E = S
ı∈I Eı is a disjoint union of chains indexed by a finite or countable
index set I, and the set of vertical labels F = S
ı∈I Fı is a disjoint union indexed
by the same index set, where each set Fı is a singleton set or a two-point link.
(2) The set of X is endowed with a partial order ≥setting x ≥y in X if and only if
there exists an index ı ∈I such that x, y ∈Eı and x ≥y in Eı.
(3) The relation ≈is an equivalence relation on the set X such that
• each equivalence class has cardinality one or two,
• any element from a two-point link is only equivalent to itself,
• no horizontal label is equivalent to a vertical label.
We will abbreviate a bunch of skew-gentle type (X, ≥, ≈) by X.
Remark 7.2. The reduction method of the present article allows to reduce the classifica-
tion problem of the right-bounded derived category D−(mod A) of the arrow ideal comple-
tion of the path algebra A of any skew-gentle quiver, which may have oriented cycles, to a
matrix problem of a bunch of skew-gentle type. This motivates the terminology above.
The following datum will serve as running example for this section.
Example 7.3. Assume that X0 = E ∪F = (E1 ∪E2) ∪(F1 ∪F2) is given by the disjoint
union of the chains E1 = {a1 < a2 < a3} and E2 = {b1 > b2 > b3}, and two-point links
F1 = {f+, f−} and F2 = {g+, g−}. Let ≈be the unique equivalence relation on the set X
satisfying a1 ≈b1 and a2 ≈b2. Then X0 is a bunch of skew-gentle type.
For the remainder of this section, we fix a bunch of skew-gentle type X and a field k.
For simplicity of the presentation, we assume that k is algebraically closed. The case of
an arbitrary field will be addressed in Remark 7.27.
Definition 7.4.

• For any elements b, y ∈F with b ≈y it holds that τbb = τyy.

(3) The direct sum of k-linear representations µ and µ′ of X is defined by (µı ⊕µ′
ı)ı∈I.
(4) A k-linear representation µ of X is called trivial if all of its vector spaces are zero,
indecomposable if it is not isomorphic to a direct sum of non-trivial representa-
tions, and regular if each k-linear map µı is an isomorphism.
The k-linear representations of a bunch of skew-gentle type X and their morphisms
form an additive, k-linear category rep X, which has the Krull-Remak-Schmidt property
by [23]. We are interested in the problem to classify the indecomposable objects in the full
subcategory of rep X given by regular representations, which will be denoted by rep∼
=(X).
Choosing bases in each k-linear representation of a bunch of skew-gentle type X allows to
rephrase this classification problem for rep∼
=(X) in terms of matrices.
Remark 7.5. A regular k-linear matrix representation of X can be described by a family
of invertible block matrices M = (Mı)ı∈I of the following form.
• For each index ı ∈I the matrix Mı has entries from the field k and finitely many
rows and columns.
• For each index ı ∈I the rows of the matrix Mı are divided into horizontal stripes
which are labeled by elements from Eı. Similarly, the columns of the matrix Mı
are divided into vertical stripes labeled by elements from Fı.
• For any distinct elements x, y ∈X such that x ≈y the horizontal stripes labeled by
x and y have the same number of rows.
Two matrix representations M and M ′ of the bunch of skew-gentle type X are isomorphic
if and only if M can be obtained from M′ by a sequence of transformations of any of the
following types.
(1) Independent transformations within stripes. Let x ∈X be an element which is
only equivalent to itself. Let ı ∈I denote the index such that x ∈Eı ∪Fı.
• If x ∈Eı, we may perform any elementary transformation of rows in the
horizontal stripe labeled by x.
• If x ∈Fı, we may perform any elementary transformation of columns in the
vertical stripe corresponding to x.
(2) Simultaneous transformations of equivalent stripes. Assume that x, y ∈X are two
distinct, equivalent elements. Then either x ∈Eı and y ∈Eȷ or x ∈Fı and y ∈Fȷ
for certain indices ı, ȷ ∈I.
• In the first case, we may perform any elementary transformation of rows in the
horizontal stripe with label x in the matrix Mı and the same transformation
of rows in the horizontal stripe with label y in the matrix Mȷ.
• Similarly, in the second case, we may perform any elementary column trans-
formation in the vertical stripe with label x in the matrix Mı and the same
column transformation in the vertical stripe with label y in the matrix Mȷ.
(3) Independent transformations between stripes. Let x, y ∈X with x < y. Then
x, y ∈Eı with ı ∈I. For any λ ∈k we may add the λ-multiple of any row of the
horizontal stripe with label x to any row of the horizontal stripe with label y.
The notions of direct sum and indecomposability of representations translate into matrix
language in a straightforward way.
Using this translation, the problem to classify the
indecomposable objects in rep∼
=(X) can be formulated as a matrix problem, that is, a problem
of linear algebra.

7.2. Alphabet of a bunch of skew-gentle type. We recall that we have fixed a bunch
of skew-gentle type X.
Definition 7.9. We associate a new datum (AX, ≥, ∼, −) to X where AX is called the set
of letters of X endowed with a partial order ≥and symmetric relations ∼and −.
• The set AX is obtained by ‘collapsing each two-point link’ in the set X as follows.
Each two-point link Fı = {fı,+, fı,−} in the union F = S
ı Fı is replaced by a
singleton set ¯Fı = {fı}. This yields new sets ¯F and AX = E ∪¯F.
• The set AX inherits the partial order from X, that is, two distinct elements x, y ∈
AX are comparable if and only if x, y ∈Eı for an index ı ∈I.
• An element f ∈AX obtained from collapsing a two-point link is called special. For
any special element f we set f ∼f. Any distinct elements x, y ∈X with x ≈y in
X exist also in AX, and we set x ∼y. In this case, each of the symbols x and y is
called tied.
• For any x, y ∈AX we set x −y if there is an index ı ∈I such that x ∈Eı and
y ∈¯Fı or vice versa.
We will abbreviate (AX, ≥, ∼, −) by AX.
Example 7.10. In case of the bunch X0 from Example 7.3 the set of letters is given by
AX0 = E1 ∪E2 ∪{f} ∪{g}, the symmetric relation ∼on AX0 is generated by a1 ∼b1,
a2 ∼b2, f ∼f, g ∼g, and the symmetric relation −by f −e1 for any e1 ∈E1 and g −e2
for any e2 ∈E2. In particular, the elements a3 and b3 are neither tied nor special.

Definition 7.16. A string of X is given by one of the following.
(1) A usual string is given by a usual, asymmetric word w.
(2) A special string (w, ε) is given by a special word w and a sign ε ∈{+, −}.
(3) A bispecial string (ε1, w, ε2) is given by a bispecial word w and signs ε1, ε2 ∈
{+, −}.

7.3. Matrix representations of bands and strings. Throughout this subsection, let
ω be a string or band of X and let w denote its underlying word. The next goal is to define
a k-linear representation M(ω) of X. This construction requires several preparatory steps
involving mostly the word w.
(1) Each special letter f is changed into f+ or f−.
(2) Each subword f+ ∼f−or f−∼f+ in w is decorated by a horizontal arrow.
(3) In the final step, each horizontal arrow and each relation −in w give rise to
non-zero entries in the matrix representation M(ω).
Each of these steps is carried out in one of the next subsections. The first step determines
the vector spaces in the representation M(ω), while the second determines its matrices.
In fact, the first two steps can be interchanged and the second one is the more technical.
7.3.1. Signs of special letters. In case the word w is bispecial, we need first to identify its
‘smallest asymmetric’ part for the first step.
Definition 7.18.
(1) Given a bispecial word v of length ℓof X and a number m ∈N,
the m-th power of v is defined as the word vm = v ∼vop ∼v ∼vop . . . ∼of length
ℓm which ends with v if m is odd, respectively with vop if m is even.
(2) The primitive root of a bispecial word w of X is the shortest possible subword v
satisfying w = vm for a number m ∈N, which is called the multiplicity of v in w.
Remark 7.19. As the formulation suggests, any bispecial word of X has a unique primitive
root with a well-defined multiplicity. A bispecial word w is symmetric if and only if its
primitive root has even multiplicity in w. In this sense, primitive roots may be viewed as
bispecial words satisfying a stronger notion of asymmetry.
With the above preparations, each special letter of w is given a sign as follows.
• If ω is not a bispecial string, any special subword f ∼f is changed to f+ ∼f−.
• If ω is a special string (w, ±) then the special end f of w is changed to f±.
• If ω is a bispecial string (ε1, w, ε2), then w = v ∼vop ∼v . . . vm is the m-th power
of its primitive root v for certain m ∈N.

ε2.
– In every copy of vop in w, we choose the opposite sign ε2 of ε2 at its left end

Thus, the right end of w obtains sign ε2 if m is odd, respectively ε1 if m is even.
The resulting ‘polarized’ word is a sequence of relations and labels from the set underlying

7.5. Bondarenko’s classification. Some bands and strings yield isomorphic represen-
tations. This motivates the next notion. For simplicity of presentation, throughout this
subsection we make an additional assumption on the bunch of skew-gentle type X.
for any periodic word w of X, the number of oriented arrows in
↔w is even.
(*)
This assumption is satisfied by the example X0 as well as the later case of interest XΛ.
Definition 7.24. Equivalence on the set of bands and strings of a bunch of skew-gentle
type X satisfying (*) is defined as the equivalence relation generated by the following.
(1) Any band (w, m, λ) is equivalent to the bands (w2, m, 1
λ) and (wop, m, λ±), where
λ± = λ if w is asymmetric and λ± = 1
λ otherwise.
(2) Any usual string w is equivalent to the usual string wop.
(3) Any special string (w, ε) is equivalent to the special string (wop, ε).
(4) Any bispecial string (ε1, w, ε2) is equivalent to the bispecial string (ε2, (vop)m, ε1),
where v denotes the primitive root of the bispecial word w and m its multiplicity.
The following statement follows from work by Bondarenko [4, 5].
Theorem 7.25. Let X be any bunch of skew-gentle type satisfying (*) and k an alge-
braically closed field. Then the following statements hold.
(1) For any string or band ω the corresponding representation M(ω) is a regular in-
decomposable k-linear representation of X.


## Page 52


52
IGOR BURBAN AND WASSILIJ GNEDIN
(2) Any indecomposable regular k-linear representation M is isomorphic to the repre-
sentation M(ω) for a suitable string or band ω of X.
(3) Any two strings or bands ω and ω′ of X are equivalent if and only if their corre-
sponding representations M(ω) and M(ω′) are isomorphic.
In brief terms, the theorem states that there is a bijection between the set of equiv-
alence classes of strings and bands of X and the set of isomorphism classes of regular
indecomposable k-linear representations of X.
Remark 7.26. In the setup of Theorem 7.25, the canonical representations of some bands
and strings of X admit the following isomorphic presentations.
(1) For any band ω = (w, m, λ) the representation M(ω) is isomorphic to the repre-
sentation with the Jordan block Jλ in M(ω) replaced by any similar matrix.
(2) Let ω be a symmetric band or a bispecial string with multiplicity larger than one.
Let M′(ω) denote the representation defined in the same way as M(ω) except that
we use the opposite orientation −−−−−−→
xi ∼xi+1 at each axis of symmetry in w.
(a) If ω is a symmetric band (w, m, λ), the representation M′(ω) is isomorphic
to the representation M(ω′) for the band ω′ = (w, m, 1
λ).
(b) If ω is bispecial string (ε1, w, ε2) with multiplicity m > 1, the representation
M′(ω) is isomorphic to M(ω′) for the bispecial string ω′ = ω if m is odd and
ω′ = (ε1, w, ε2) if m is even.
Remark 7.27. Assume that k is an arbitrary field. Then Definitions 7.13 and 7.24 have
to be adapted as follows.
• In Definition 7.13, a band is a triple (w, m, p) where w is a cyclic word, m ∈N
and p ∈k[x] is a monic irreducible polynomial such that p(x) ̸= x and also p(x) ̸=
x + (−1)t(w) if w is symmetric.
• In Definition 7.24, any band (w, m, p) is equivalent to the bands (w2, m, ep), where
ep(x) =
1

x) denotes the reciprocal monic polynomial of p, as well as
(wop, m, p±) with p± = p if w is asymmetric and p± = ep otherwise.
• In Subsection 7.3.3, we replace the Jordan block Jλ of size m in the band represen-
tation M(ω) with the Frobenius block Φpm with minimal polynomial pm.
With these changes, Theorem 7.25 remains valid over the arbitrary field k.
We give a few more remarks for context, which are not essential later on.
(1) A bunch of gentle type X is a bunch of skew-gentle type together with the require-
ment that there are no two-point links. A similar statement as Remark 7.2 is true
for gentle quivers. In this case, the steps described in Subsections 7.3.1 and 7.3.2
are void and the construction of canonical forms M(ω) is considerably simpler.
(2) The notion of a bunch of semichains X can be obtained from the notion of a bunch
of skew-gentle type via the following modifications.
• Horizontal labels are allowed to be equivalent to vertical labels.
• For any index ı each of the set Eı or Fı is allowed to be a subposet of the
‘infinite semi-chain’ ({i+, i−| i ∈Z}, ≤) with i± < j± and i± < j∓if i < j.
(3) There are extensions of the notions of finite words, strings and their associated
canonical forms which provide a complete classification of indecomposable, not
necessarily regular representations of any bunch of semichains X.

which are neither bispecial strings nor bands.
8. Band and string complexes of the Gelfand order
In this section, we introduce sequences of numerical data called periodic and finite
words, which are the main ingredients to define bands and strings of Db(rep Λ). Words are
visualized in terms of gluing diagrams. For any band or string ω we construct a complex
X•(ω) in Db(rep Λ). The gluing diagram associated to ω is processed in three steps: vertices
are replaced by projective Λ-modules, gluing edges are oriented via a technically involved
symmetry rule in an ambient word, oriented edges then induce additional Λ-linear maps
in the gluing diagram. The outcome is summarized in the compact form of a cycle or
line diagram in Subsection 8.2.4. Such a diagram yields directly the complex X•(ω). We
give examples of the construction of band and string complexes in Subsection 8.3, and
formulate one of the main results of this part of the article in Theorem 8.21. Its proof
is based on the categorical reductions of the preceding three sections and deferred until
the next section. The latter also clarifies the connection between the notion of bands
and strings of the bunch XΛ in the sense of Section 7 and the notions of the present,
self-contained section.
8.1. Bands and strings of indecomposable projective complexes. As before, we
assume that the base field k is algebraically closed.
Arbitrary fields are addressed in
Remark 8.26. We are going to define periodic words / bands / finite words / strings of
Db(rep Λ). For brevity, we will omit that these notions are meant to be associated to
Db(rep Λ), and use the more precise wording when it will become relevant in the next
section.
We introduce a set of formal symbols called oriented numbers

Definition 8.7. There are three classes of strings defined as follows.
(1) A usual string (d, w) is given by an integer d ∈Z and a usual asymmetric word w.
(2) A special string (d, w, ε) is given by an integer d ∈Z , any special word w, and a
sign ε ∈{+, −}.
(3) A bispecial string (d, ε1, w, ε2) is given by an integer d ∈Z, any bispecial word w,
and two signs ε1, ε2 ∈{+, −}.
8.2. Definition of band and string complexes. Throughout this subsection, we fix a
band or string ω, that is, ω is a a band (d, w, m, λ), or a string of the form (d, w), (d, w, ε)
or (d, ε1, w, ε2). The next goal is to describe the construction of a bounded complex X•(ω)
in the category Db(rep Λ). In terms of the gluing diagram of w, this construction is divided
into the following steps.
(1) Each vertex in the gluing diagram is attributed a certain degree d ∈Z and gives
rise to a projective Λ-module.
(2) Each edge in the gluing diagram obtains an orientation.
(3) The oriented edges induce new arrows in the gluing diagram.
Each step corresponds to one of the next subsections. Step (1) determines already the
projective module Xi(ω) in X•(ω) at each degree i ∈Z. Step (2) is technically involved
and depends only on the sequence of oriented numbers in the word w. Steps (2) and (3)
determine the differentials of the complex X•(ω).
8.2.1. Vertices and projectives. Before we can carry out the first step, we need a few
notions concerning degrees and bispecial words.
Definition 8.8. Let (d, w) be a pair, where d ∈Z and w is a periodic word with ˙w =
(x1, x2, . . . , xℓ) or w is a finite word (α, x1, x2, . . . , xℓ, β).
(1) The degree sequence associated to (d, w) is the sequence (d0, d1, . . . , dℓ) defined
inductively by d0 = d and di = di−1 −1 if xi is right oriented, respectively di =
di−1 + 1 if xi is left oriented for each index 1 ≤i ≤ℓ.
(2) The opposite degree of the pair (d, w) is set to dop := dℓ.
(3) The support of (d, w) is the set of integers supp(d, w) = {d0, d1, . . . , dℓ}.
Definition 8.9. Below, we consider bispecial words in the sense of Definition 8.4.
(1) For a bispecial word v we denote by ˙v = (x1, x2, . . . xℓ) its underlying sequence of
oriented numbers and set ˙vop := (xℓ, . . . x2, x1). For any number m ∈N the m-th
power of ˙v is defined as the concatenation of sequences ˙vm = ˙v◦˙vop ◦˙v◦˙vop . . .◦˙vm
which ends with ˙vm = ˙v if m is odd, respectively ˙vm = ˙vop if m is even.

multiplicity of the primitive root in w.
In this step, vertices in the gluing diagram are replaced by projective Λ-modules.

Orientation mnemonic in terms of gluing diagrams.
The orientation rule (8.11) for the
gluing edge κi at degree di has the following diagrammatic interpretation.
(1) The word w admits a gluing diagram of the form (8.2) or (8.5) but possibly with
half integers as decorations.
(2) Starting from the gluing edge κi we read the diagonal arrows one step to the
left, and one step to the right. If these arrows have the same numerical values
and opposite orientations we contract the subdiagram containing the two squares
containing these to a single gluing edge denoted again by κi.

In each case, the flip of the orientation would destroy the desired sink
or source, and create a transition vertex. Therefore the orientation is
unique. In each case, there is a source and sink incident to the diagonal

The edge κi in the gluing diagram of w is oriented in the same direction as
the processed edge above.
This diagrammatic procedure recovers the rules (8.11). A brief mnemonic for the dia-
grammatic orientation rules is that the orientation of κi avoids transitions through smaller
values.
8.2.3. Induced arrows and homomorphisms. So far, we have replaced the vertices by pro-
jectives and oriented all gluing edges in the gluing diagram of the band or string ω.
The next rules provide new arrows depending on the particular orientations of the gluing
edges. As these rules do not depend on the isomorphism class of the projective at each
vertex, any projective P⋆, P+ or P−will be denoted simply by P in the diagrams below.

Folding.
At last, we ‘fold’ the gluing diagram in the following sense.
(1) All vertical gluing edges are ignored.
(2) For any degree i ∈Z we define a projective Xi as the direct sum of all projectives
which were attributed degree i in the diagram.
(3) For each degree i ∈Z the decorations of the arrows in the gluing diagram starting
at projectives of degree i yield all non-zero entries of a map ∂i : Xi
Xi−1.

such that [a, b] = supp(d, w). It is straightforward to check that ∂i+1∂i = 0 for each degree
i ∈Z, that is, X•(ω) is a complex in Db(rep Λ).

Compressed notation for bispecial strings.
Assume that ω is a non-primitive bispecial
string (d, ε1, w, ε2). Let m denote the multiplicity of the primitive root v of w. On the
one hand, the complex X•(ω) admits a presentation like (8.15) but with certain changes of
signs in the intermediate projectives as described in Subsection 8.2.3. On the other hand,
the complex X•(ω) admits a more compact notation involving block matrices of size m,
which is similar to those of bands and described next.
For any sign ε ∈{+, −} we define a projective Λ-module P (m,ε) as the alternating

At last, we obtain the band complex X•(ω) on the right. This example indicates that band
complexes can be described more generally for periodic words with arbitrary numerical
values using coefficient matrices which reflect all possible orientations of gluing edges. A
similar observation holds true for string complexes.
8.4. Main gluing result. By the last subsection, any band and any string provides a
complex in Db(rep Λ).
However, some bands or strings may yield complexes that are
isomorphic in the derived category. This motivates the next notion.
Definition 8.20. We consider the smallest equivalence relation on the set of bands and
strings of Db(rep Λ) such that the following holds.
(1) Any usual string (d, w) is equivalent to (dop, wop).
(2) Any special string (d, w, ε) is equivalent to (dop, wop, ε).
(3) Any bispecial string (d, ε1, w, ε2) is equivalent to (dop, ε2, (vop)m, ε1), where v de-
notes the primitive root of w and m its multiplicity.
(4) Any band (d, w, m, λ) is equivalent to (d1, w1, m, λ) as well as (d, wop, m, λ∓) where
λ∓= 1
λ if w is asymmetric, and λ∓= λ otherwise.
In particular, any string is not equivalent to any band or a string of another of the three
classes. The main result of this section is the following.

9. Proof of the Gluing Theorem
The goal of this section is to prove Theorem 8.21 on the correspondence of bands and
strings to indecomposable complexes. The proof has three steps.
(1) First, we observe that any band or non-trivial string ω of XΛ in the sense of Subsec-
tion 8.2 admits an expansion to a band or string of XΛ as defined in Subsection 7.2.

9.3. Construction of pullback complexes of bands and strings.
Definition 9.13. For a band or string ω of Db(rep Λ), the pullback complex P•(ω) is the
image under the composition of bijections in (9.1).
In more detail, the pullback complex is defined by the following steps.
• If ω is a trivial string, then ω is equivalent to (d, (⋆,

In particular, P•(ω) is an indecomposable projective complex of Db(rep Λ).

10. Action of natural functors on band and string complexes
The derived category Db(rep Λ) of nilpotent Gelfand quiver representations admits three
natural auto-equivalences: the involution σ, the Auslander-Reiten translation τ and the
contragredient duality (−)‡. In the present section, we express these functors in terms
of basic operations on the gluing diagram of a band or string ω of Db(rep Λ) such as the
half-turn, the transpose of each arrow, and the replacement of each projective P⋆by the
gluing diagram of the projective resolution of I⋆. The diagrammatic interpretation allows
to describe the action of the three natural auto-equivalences τ, σ and (−)‡ on bands and
strings of Db(rep Λ). Moreover, we give a homological characterization of the four classes
of band and string complexes. The statements of the results of this section presume only
familiarity with the notions of bands and strings from Section 8.

lows to deduce a purely homological characterization of the four combinatorially defined
classes of bands and strings in terms of the involution and the defect. As introduced in

Theorem 10.2. Let ω be a band or string of Db(rep Λ) and X• = X•(ω) its corresponding
indecomposable complex. Then the following equivalences hold.
ω is a usual string

Proof. By the definitions, δ(X•) is given by the number of non-special ends in the word
underlying the datum ω. Therefore, ω is a usual string if and only if δ(X•) = 2, a special
string if and only if δ(X•) = 1, and a bispecial string or a band if and only if δ(X•) = 0.
The equivalences follow then from Propositions 10.1 and 4.21.
□
We note that any complex X• of Db(rep Λ) with defect greater than two must be de-
composable.

11. Indecomposable projective resolutions via gluing triples
In the present section, we address the question which band and string complexes are
actually projective resolutions. Throughout the rest of this article, the term projective
resolution means always a projective resolution of a module.
The answer is formulated in Theorem 11.6 in homological and combinatorial terms, as
well as an enumeration of the relevant bands and strings in Table 11.7. The proof of the
theorem uses the realization from Subsection 5.1 of a complex X• in Db(rep Λ) as pullback
of a certain gluing triple (S•, Y•, ϑ). In the first subsection, we formulate simple necessary
conditions for X• to be a projective resolution in terms of the normalization complex Y•.
This restricts the list of candidates for projective resolutions severely. It turns out that
any candidate complex qualifies as a projective resolution, because it is quasi-isomorphic
to a ‘syzygy resolution’ by which we mean a two-term complex of the form (L
P0)
with projective module P0.
The last subsection derives a formula for the Jordan–H¨older-multiplicities of the non-
trivial homology of a projective resolution in terms of the complexes S• and Y•.

We note the following characterization of gluing triples corresponding to projective
resolution which follows from results in [19, Section 3.3.1].
Remark 11.4. The complex X• is a projective resolution if and only if condition (c) holds,
2 dim Sj = dim Tj for all j ≥2 and im ϑ∗
n ∩im Hn(ηY•) = 0 for all n ≥1.
However, checking the last condition of the characterization in practice involves linear
algebra computations or a truncation argument. Instead of using this characterization, we
will show that any indecomposable complex satisfying (c) of Proposition 11.3, which is a
‘candidate’ for a projective resolution, does qualify as such by a reduction to a complex
satisfying (a), and thus (b).
11.2. Bands and strings of projective resolutions. In this section, we leave the cat-
egorical language of gluing triples and make use of the explicit combinatorics of band and
string complexes provided by Subsection 8.2.
The following description of the normalization of a band or string complex was already
given implicitly.
Lemma 11.5. Let ω be a band or string of Db(rep Λ), and X•(ω) the corresponding band
respectively string complex. Then the complex Γ⊗ΛX•(ω) is isomorphic to the direct sum of
two-term complexes obtained from the polarized gluing diagram of ω from Subsection 8.2.1
by deleting all gluing edges, replacing P± by eP⋄, and P⋆by eP⋆.
Proof. As F(X•(ω)) ∼= FGI(µ(ω)) ∼= I(µ(ω)) by Proposition 9.19 and Theorem 5.16, it
follows that Γ ⊗Λ X•(ω) is isomorphic to the complex Y• described in Remark 9.14. The
latter coincides with the description in the claim.
□

12. Band and string resolutions of the Gelfand quiver
In this section, we introduce bands and strings of rep Λ as combinatorial data which
encode minimal projective resolutions. The main result of this section (Theorem 12.29)
yields a bijection between bands and strings of rep Λ and indecomposable projective res-
olutions in Db(rep Λ).
The proof is based on Theorem 11.6 together with the explicit
description of band and string complexes of Subsection 8.2. For a band or string ω the
corresponding resolution X•(ω) can be viewed as gluing of projective resolutions of cyclic
finite-dimensional Λ-modules. These projective resolutions are precisely the direct sum-
mands of the cyclification of X•(ω) introduced in Subsection 12.5, which will be useful in
the next sections.

12.2. Band resolutions. Next, we will modify the definitions of periodic words and
bands of Db(rep Λ) in order to define counterparts for rep Λ. For readers familiar with
Subsection 8.1, we have highlighted the modifications in the new definitions. Bands of
rep Λ will be translated in certain diagrams.
The goal of this is to define a projective resolution X•(ω) for each band ω of rep Λ. The
main technical step is to transform the underlying word w into a datum w↕containing a
vertical arrow ↑or ↓between any two successive oriented numbers.

Definition 12.17. There are three classes of strings of rep Λ defined as follows.
(1) A usual string w is given by a usual asymmetric word w.
(2) A special string (w, ε) is given by any special word w, and a sign ε ∈{+, −}.
(3) A bispecial string (ε1, w, ε2) is given by any bispecial word w, and signs ε1, ε2 ∈
{+, −}.
In each case, w denotes a finite word of rep Λ.

of Definition 8.7 as follows.

12.4. Bijection theorem. Isomorphisms of certain band complexes or string complexes
motivate the next notion.
Definition 12.27. We consider the smallest equivalence relation on the set of bands and
strings of rep Λ such that the following holds.
• Any usual string w is equivalent to wop.
• Any special string (w, ε) is equivalent to (wop, ε).
• Any bispecial string (ε1, w, ε2) is equivalent to (ε2, (vop)m, ε1), where v denotes the
primitive root of w and m its multiplicity.
• Any band (w, m, λ) is equivalent to (w1, m, λ) as well as (wop, m, λ∓) with λ∓= 1
λ
if w is asymmetric and λ∓= λ otherwise.

For an object M of rep Λ we denote its dimension vector by dim M = (dim Mv)v∈{−,⋆,+}.
Remark 12.33. As rep Λ is a finite length category, for each vertex v ∈Q0 the positive
integer dim Mv is equal to and can be interpreted as the multiplicity of the simple module
Sv in any Jordan–H¨older filtration of the Λ-module M. Therefore, the dimension vector
records the Jordan–H¨older multiplicities of M.
Proposition 12.34. For any band or string ω of rep Λ the module Mcyc(ω) is a direct
sum of cyclic Λ-modules such that dim M(ω) = dim Mcyc(ω).
Proof. By definition, the complex Xcyc
•
(ω) is a direct sum of complexes of the form (12.2),
which are minimal projective resolutions of cyclic modules. As the complexes X•(ω) and
Xcyc
•
(ω) have identical projective at each degree, it holds that A⊗ΛX•(ω) ∼= A⊗ΛXcyc
•
(ω).
The complex Γ ⊗Γ X•(ω) is isomorphic to Γ ⊗Γ Xcyc
•
(ω) by Lemma 11.5.
Therefore,
Corollary 11.10 can be applied to deduce that dim M(ω) = dim Mcyc(ω).
□
The last result yields a useful tool to determine the dimension vector of the homology of
any band or string resolution, which does not require a full description of the differentials.

13. Invariants and functorial properties of band and string
representations
With the description of band and string resolutions in hand, we describe their behaviour
under the involution σ and the contragredient duality (−)‡ in Theorem 13.2 and identify
the self-dual resolutions (Corollary 13.3). Moreover, we compute projective and injective
dimension, top, socle, dimension vector, and Euler characteristic of the indecomposable
band or string representation M(ω) in rep Λ directly from the numerical data of ω (The-
orem 13.5, Table 13.9 and Corollary 13.13). In different terms, these invariants depend
only on the much coarser cyclification Mcyc(ω), that is, on the ‘cyclic ingredients’ in the
gluing of M(ω).

interpretation in terms of gluing edges at degree one.
• Any gluing edge at degree one between arrows with support {0, 1} gives rise to a
gluing edge at degree zero for ω†.
• Any gluing edge between an arrow with support {0, 1} and an arrow with support
{1, 2} in the gluing diagram ω has no corresponding gluing edge at degree zero in
the gluing diagram of ω†.

Remark 13.10. We refer to the invariants in Theorem 13.5 as ‘numerical invariants’
below and note the following.
(1) The statements on the projective and injective dimension recover Proposition 4.12.
(2) The number of generators of M(ω) as Λ-module is given by ℓµ.
(3) The modules M(ω) and Mcyc(ω) share the same numerical invariants.
(4) Reordering the numbers without change of orientations in the word underlying a
band or string ω does not change the numerical invariants of M(ω).
(5) To any band ω = (w, m, λ) with ˙w = (xi)ℓ
i=1 we may associate a bispecial string
ω′ = (−, w′, +) setting w′ = (xi)mℓ
i=1. Then the band representation M(ω) and
the bispecial string representation M(ω′) have the same numerical invariants. It
appears that M(ω) and M(ω′) can be distinguished only via the involution σ; the

hold.
(1) It holds that |χ(M, N)| ≤2.
(2) Let ω be the band or string of rep Λ such that M ∼= M(ω). Then the following

• χ(M) = 2 if and only if ω is one of the following;
– ω is a usual string w with (β2⋆, β1⋆, β0⋆) ∈{(1, 1, 0), (0, 1, 1)},
– ω is a bispecial string (ε1, w, ε2) such that the length of w is odd and
ε1 ̸= ε2,
– ω is a bispecial string (ε1, w, ε2) such that the length of w is even, ε1 = ε2
and the primitive root of w has odd multiplicity.
• χ(M) = 1 if and only if ω is a special string.
• χ(M) = 0 if and only if ω is a band or another string than the ones above.

14. Explicit band and string representations
We recall that a nilpotent representation of the Gelfand quiver is given by a diagram
of finite-dimensional k-linear spaces and k-linear maps

In this section, we give an explicit description of the indecomposable quiver representation
M(ω) associated to each band or string ω of rep Λ. This section requires notions from the
previous one. Except for an argument providing the dimension of M(ω), the proofs of this
section are self-contained. Some of the examples include gluing diagrams with induced
arrows. The precise rules for induction of arrows have been described in Subsection 8.2.3,
though reviewing these technicalities is not necessary when taking the band and string
resolutions from Subsections 12.2 and 12.3 as given. The explicit representation matrices
of bands are described in Propositions 14.8, 14.14, those for strings in Proposition 14.21.

14.2. Explicit band and string representations. To deduce that a certain candidate
set B is a basis of M(ω) we will use the following simple observation.
Lemma 14.7. Let ω be a band or string of rep Λ and M = M(ω) its band or string
representation.
A subset B of M is a k-linear basis of M if it satisfies the following
conditions.
(1) The set B contains a set of Λ-linear generators of M.
(2) The subspace U := ⟨B⟩k is a Λ-submodule of M, or, equivalently,
• any element x ∈B is contained in evM for some vertex v ∈Q0.
• any element x± ∈e±B satisfies B±x± ∈U, and
• any element x⋆∈e⋆B satisfies A+x⋆, A−x⋆∈U.
(3) It holds that |B| = dim Mcyc(ω).
Proof. As M is finite-dimensional, so is U and there is a number n ∈N with radn U = 0.
The assumptions on B imply that M = ΛU = (Λ/ radn Λ)U = U/ radn U = U. The third
property and Proposition 12.34 imply that |B| = dim M, and thus B is a basis.
□

Lemma 14.13. For the band ω as above, the following statements hold.
(1) There is an integer i ∈Z such that pi ̸= qi or γi = 0.
(2) Similarly, there is an integer j ∈Z such that pj ̸= qj or γj = 0.
Proof. We recall that the sequences (pi)i∈Z, (qi)i∈Z, (γi)i∈Z (γi)i∈Z are k-periodic.
To
show (1), assume that pi = qi and γi ̸= 0 for any i ∈Z. Then βi ̸= 0, which implies that
qi ≤pi+1 by Remark 12.13, for any i ∈Z. As w is 2k-periodic with k ≥2 by assumption,
it follows that all numbers in p1 = q1 ≤p2 = q2 ≤. . . pk = qk ≤p1 are equal. Therefore,
w has period two, a contradiction, which shows the first claim.
The second claim is proven similarly, using that γj ̸= 0 only if pj+1 ≥qj+1.
□
From now, we omit the standard relations involving idempotents, assuming that any
vectors with the letter u or x belong to M−(ω), vectors with letters v or y to M⋆(ω), and
vectors with letters w or z to M+(ω).
